Well, I guess the main thing is I just can’t keep my mind on one thing lately, and it’s starting to cause problems at work. I’m in the warehouse, so there’s always noise and movement, you know—beepers going off, the forklift backing up, someone dragging or moving a pallet—and every little thing pulls me away from what I’m doing.

Like, I’ll be counting inventory, and I really am trying to focus on the numbers, but then one alarm goes off or someone walks by with something, and my attention just snaps over there. Then I come back and I’ve lost my place, or I think I remember where I was, but later I realize I put the wrong numbers in. It’s embarrassing, because I know how to do my job. I don’t know that I’d call it depressed, exactly. I mean, I get frustrated, definitely, especially when I mess something up that I know I should be able to do. That can make me feel pretty low in the moment, like I’m irritated with myself or kind of worn out by it.

I haven’t really been thinking of it as depression. It’s more that I’m bothered and stressed because I can’t keep my focus where it’s supposed to be, and then I start worrying about what people at work think.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find yourself worrying a lot, even when there isn’t an immediate problem to deal with?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 57 / laborer_or_freight_stock_or_material_mover / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know… I wouldn’t say I’m someone who’s worrying all day for no reason, exactly. I mean, I do worry about work right now because the mistakes are real, and I know if I keep losing my place or putting in the wrong numbers, that could become a problem. So that feels like there is something to worry about, if that makes sense.

But when there isn’t anything going on? I’m not sure. I don’t sit around thinking, like, “What if the whole world falls apart?” or anything like that. I can get stuck thinking things over, but it’s usually tied to something that actually happened, like replaying whether I counted something wrong or whether somebody noticed. I guess I’m not sure where the line is between normal worry and “worrying a lot.”

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not totally sure how to judge that. Part of me thinks, well, it’s a big routine change, so maybe it makes sense that I’d feel thrown off. I was used to having the house and the day work a certain way, and now it’s just different all the time, even if nothing “bad” is happening.

But then another part of me wonders if I’m reacting more strongly than I should, because it’s not like he did something terrible by being home. I don’t want it to sound like I don’t want him around, because that’s not really it. It’s more like I feel more unsettled and irritated than I expected I would, and then I feel guilty about feeling that way.

So… I don’t know. Maybe a little stronger than I would’ve expected, but I can’t say for sure. It’s hard to measure that.

I don’t want it to sound like I don’t want him around, because that’s not really it. It’s more like I feel more unsettled and irritated than I expected I would, and then I feel guilty about feeling that way.\n\nSo… I don’t know. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I honestly don’t know if they would mostly go away. I think having the old routine back would probably help some, because I wouldn’t feel as thrown off at home, and maybe I’d go into work feeling less rattled.

But the focus problem itself is what really brought me in, because it’s been happening at the warehouse in a way that feels bigger than just being annoyed about my husband being home. Like, I’ll be doing a count, and then a beeper goes off, or a forklift alarm, or somebody moves a pallet nearby, and my mind just snaps over to that. Then I lose the thread of what number I was on, and later I realize I entered the wrong numbers in inventory.

So… maybe the routine going back would take some pressure off, yes. But I’m not sure it would fix it completely, because the problem is that I can’t seem to keep my mind on one thing the way I normally can.

It’s like instead of settling into this new phase with him being home, we’re sort of walking around each other. I’ll get irritated, or he’ll say something, and then it turns into this back-and-forth when it probably didn’t need to. So yes, it’s not just work. It’s spilling over at home too.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your distress about your husband being home is stronger than you would expect for that change?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to judge that. Part of me thinks, well, it’s a big routine change, so maybe it makes sense that I’d feel thrown off. I was used to having the house and the day work a certain way, and now it’s just different all the time, even if nothing “bad” is happening.\n\nBut then another part of me wonders if I’m reacting more strongly than I should, because it’s not like he did something terrible by being home. I don’t want it to sound like I don’t want him around, because that’s not really it. It’s more like I feel more unsettled and irritated than I expected I would, and then I feel guilty about feeling that way.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Maybe a little stronger than I would’ve expected, but I can’t say for sure. It’s hard to measure that.\n\nDoctor: If your husband went back to working outside the home and your old routine returned, do you think these focus problems would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know if they would mostly go away. I think having the old routine back would probably help some, because I wouldn’t feel as thrown off at home, and maybe I’d go into work feeling less rattled.\n\nBut the focus problem itself is what really brought me in, because it’s been happening at the warehouse in a way that feels bigger than just being annoyed about my husband being home. Like, I’ll be doing a count, and then a beeper goes off, or a forklift alarm, or somebody moves a pallet nearby, and my mind just snaps over to that. Then I lose the thread of what number I was on, and later I realize I entered the wrong numbers in inventory.\n\nSo… maybe the routine going back would take some pressure off, yes. But I’m not sure it would fix it completely, because the problem is that I can’t seem to keep my mind on one thing the way I normally can.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been sleeping poorly lately?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I don’t want it to sound like I don’t want him around, because that’s not really it. Then I lose the thread of what number I was on, and later I realize I entered the wrong numbers in inventory.\n\nSo… maybe the routine going back would take some pressure off, yes. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Maybe a little, but not enough that I’d say it solves it. If he’s out of the house most of the day, I do feel less crowded or less thrown off at home, so I might start the day a bit calmer. But the thing that’s really bothering me is what happens once I’m at the warehouse.

Like I’ll be in the middle of counting something, and I’m trying to keep the number straight in my head, and then a beeper goes off, or a forklift alarm starts, or somebody shifts a pallet near me, and my attention just jumps to that before I can stop it. Then when I go back to the count, I’ve lost where I was, and later I realize I entered the wrong numbers in inventory.

So I don’t know that his being gone makes my focus “better” in the way I’d hope. It might make the day feel less irritating at home, but I still can’t seem to keep my mind on one thing the way I normally would.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess the main thing is I just can’t keep my mind on one thing lately, and it’s starting to cause problems at work. Then I come back and I’ve lost my place, or I think I remember where I was, but later I realize I put the wrong numbers in. It’s embarrassing, because I know how to do my job. It’s not like I’m new there. I just… I can’t seem to stay on the count the way I used to, and that’s why I came in.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, did you also have a hard time staying focused?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I mean, it’s been a long time since I was a kid, so I don’t know how much I’m remembering right versus just filling in blanks. I don’t have a clear memory of teachers saying, like, “she can’t focus” or anything like that.\n\nI guess I was normal enough in school? I had subjects I liked and ones I didn’t, and if I was bored I probably looked out the window like any kid. But I can’t say there was some big problem with attention back then, not that I know of. This feels more like something I’m noticing now because it’s messing with my work.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it depressed, exactly. I mean, I get frustrated, definitely, especially when I mess something up that I know I should be able to do. That can make me feel pretty low in the moment, like I’m irritated with myself or kind of worn out by it.\n\nBut if you mean like feeling sad all the time or waking up every day with that heavy feeling… I’m not sure I’d say that’s been the main thing. I haven’t really been thinking of it as depression. It’s more that I’m bothered and stressed because I can’t keep my focus where it’s supposed to be, and then I start worrying about what people at work think.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot, even when there isn’t an immediate problem to deal with?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say I’m someone who’s worrying all day for no reason, exactly. I mean, I do worry about work right now because the mistakes are real, and I know if I keep losing my place or putting in the wrong numbers, that could become a problem. So that feels like there is something to worry about, if that makes sense.\n\nBut when there isn’t anything going on? I’m not sure. I don’t sit around thinking, like, “What if the whole world falls apart?” or anything like that. I can get stuck thinking things over, but it’s usually tied to something that actually happened, like replaying whether I counted something wrong or whether somebody noticed. I guess I’m not sure where the line is between normal worry and “worrying a lot.”\n\nDoctor: Did this trouble focusing start after something particularly stressful or upsetting happened in your life?\n\nPatient: I mean… the only thing I can really connect it to is that our routine changed at home. It’s been about a month, maybe right around then, since my husband stopped working outside the home, and that kind of shifted the whole rhythm of the day.\n\nI don’t know if I’d call it “stressful” in the dramatic sense, like some huge upsetting event, but it did change things. Before, I had my usual pattern, and he had his, and now with him home more, everything feels a little different and less predictable. That’s around when I started really noticing the focus problems, especially at work. So… yes and no, I guess. Not a big crisis, but a change that threw off the normal routine.\n\nDoctor: Since your husband stopped working outside the home, have you felt more stressed than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so, though it’s hard for me to separate “stressed” from just everything feeling off, if that makes sense. It’s been going on for about a month, since he stopped working outside the home and our normal routine changed.  \n\nBefore that, we kind of had a rhythm, and I knew what the day was going to look like. Now it’s not bad exactly, but it’s different, and I do feel more tense or thrown off than usual because of it.\n\nDoctor: Besides at work, has this been causing problems in other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has. Work is the most obvious place because there are actual mistakes I can point to, like with inventory, and I’ve had to go back and double-check things with my supervisor after I realized something was off. That’s embarrassing, honestly, because I don’t like needing someone to recheck my work like that.\n\nBut it’s also been affecting home. My husband and I have been arguing more than usual, not huge blowups every time, but enough that the house feels tense. It’s like instead of settling into this new phase with him being home, we’re sort of walking around each other. I’ll get irritated, or he’ll say something, and then it turns into this back-and-forth when it probably didn’t need to. So yes, it’s not just work. It’s spilling over at home too.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your distress about your husband being home is stronger than you would expect for that change?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to judge that. Part of me thinks, well, it’s a big routine change, so maybe it makes sense that I’d feel thrown off. I was used to having the house and the day work a certain way, and now it’s just different all the time, even if nothing “bad” is happening.\n\nBut then another part of me wonders if I’m reacting more strongly than I should, because it’s not like he did something terrible by being home. I don’t want it to sound like I don’t want him around, because that’s not really it. It’s more like I feel more unsettled and irritated than I expected I would, and then I feel guilty about feeling that way.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Maybe a little stronger than I would’ve expected, but I can’t say for sure. Like, I’ll be doing a count, and then a beeper goes off, or a forklift alarm, or somebody moves a pallet nearby, and my mind just snaps over to that. Then I lose the thread of what number I was on, and later I realize I entered the wrong numbers in inventory.\n\nSo… maybe the routine going back would take some pressure off, yes. Yes, I think so. I mean, the husband thing is part of what’s been making me feel off at home, but the focusing trouble doesn’t feel like it stays only in that lane, if that makes sense.

I had subjects I liked and ones I didn’t, and if I was bored I probably looked out the window like any kid. But I can’t say there was some big problem with attention back then, not that I know of. This feels more like something I’m noticing now because it’s messing with my work.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it depressed, exactly. I mean, I get frustrated, definitely, especially when I mess something up that I know I should be able to do. That can make me feel pretty low in the moment, like I’m irritated with myself or kind of worn out by it.\n\nBut if you mean like feeling sad all the time or waking up every day with that heavy feeling… I’m not sure I’d say that’s been the main thing. I haven’t really been thinking of it as depression. It’s more that I’m bothered and stressed because I can’t keep my focus where it’s supposed to be, and then I start worrying about what people at work think.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot, even when there isn’t an immediate problem to deal with?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say I’m someone who’s worrying all day for no reason, exactly. I mean, I do worry about work right now because the mistakes are real, and I know if I keep losing my place or putting in the wrong numbers, that could become a problem. So that feels like there is something to worry about, if that makes sense.\n\nBut when there isn’t anything going on? I’m not sure. I don’t sit around thinking, like, “What if the whole world falls apart?” or anything like that. I can get stuck thinking things over, but it’s usually tied to something that actually happened, like replaying whether I counted something wrong or whether somebody noticed. I guess I’m not sure where the line is between normal worry and “worrying a lot.”\n\nDoctor: Did this trouble focusing start after something particularly stressful or upsetting happened in your life?\n\nPatient: I mean… the only thing I can really connect it to is that our routine changed at home. It’s been about a month, maybe right around then, since my husband stopped working outside the home, and that kind of shifted the whole rhythm of the day.\n\nI don’t know if I’d call it “stressful” in the dramatic sense, like some huge upsetting event, but it did change things. Before, I had my usual pattern, and he had his, and now with him home more, everything feels a little different and less predictable. That’s around when I started really noticing the focus problems, especially at work. So… yes and no, I guess. Not a big crisis, but a change that threw off the normal routine.\n\nDoctor: Since your husband stopped working outside the home, have you felt more stressed than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so, though it’s hard for me to separate “stressed” from just everything feeling off, if that makes sense. It’s been going on for about a month, since he stopped working outside the home and our normal routine changed.  \n\nBefore that, we kind of had a rhythm, and I knew what the day was going to look like. Now it’s not bad exactly, but it’s different, and I do feel more tense or thrown off than usual because of it.\n\nDoctor: Besides at work, has this been causing problems in other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has. Work is the most obvious place because there are actual mistakes I can point to, like with inventory, and I’ve had to go back and double-check things with my supervisor after I realized something was off. That’s embarrassing, honestly, because I don’t like needing someone to recheck my work like that.\n\nBut it’s also been affecting home. My husband and I have been arguing more than usual, not huge blowups every time, but enough that the house feels tense. It’s like instead of settling into this new phase with him being home, we’re sort of walking around each other. I’ll get irritated, or he’ll say something, and then it turns into this back-and-forth when it probably didn’t need to. So yes, it’s not just work. It’s spilling over at home too.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your distress about your husband being home is stronger than you would expect for that change?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to judge that. Part of me thinks, well, it’s a big routine change, so maybe it makes sense that I’d feel thrown off. I was used to having the house and the day work a certain way, and now it’s just different all the time, even if nothing “bad” is happening.\n\nBut then another part of me wonders if I’m reacting more strongly than I should, because it’s not like he did something terrible by being home. I don’t want it to sound like I don’t want him around, because that’s not really it. It’s more like I feel more unsettled and irritated than I expected I would, and then I feel guilty about feeling that way.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Maybe a little stronger than I would’ve expected, but I can’t say for sure. It’s hard to measure that.\n\nDoctor: If your husband went back to working outside the home and your old routine returned, do you think these focus problems would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know if they would mostly go away. I think having the old routine back would probably help some, because I wouldn’t feel as thrown off at home, and maybe I’d go into work feeling less rattled.\n\nBut the focus problem itself is what really brought me in, because it’s been happening at the warehouse in a way that feels bigger than just being annoyed about my husband being home. Like, I’ll be doing a count, and then a beeper goes off, or a forklift alarm, or somebody moves a pallet nearby, and my mind just snaps over to that. Then I lose the thread of what number I was on, and later I realize I entered the wrong numbers in inventory.\n\nSo… maybe the routine going back would take some pressure off, yes. But I’m not sure it would fix it completely, because the problem is that I can’t seem to keep my mind on one thing the way I normally can.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sleeping poorly lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve been sleeping poorly, exactly. I mean, I haven’t been paying close attention to it the way I probably should. Some nights feel better than others, but I can’t point to a clear pattern or say, “Yes, I’m up all night,” or anything like that.\n\nI guess with everything feeling kind of tense at home and me worrying about mistakes at work, I do notice myself thinking about things when I’m trying to settle down, but whether that means my sleep is actually bad… I don’t know. I haven’t really tracked it. So I’d say I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: On days when your husband is out of the house for most of the day, is your focus any better?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but not enough that I’d say it solves it. If he’s out of the house most of the day, I do feel less crowded or less thrown off at home, so I might start the day a bit calmer. But the thing that’s really bothering me is what happens once I’m at the warehouse.\n\nLike I’ll be in the middle of counting something, and I’m trying to keep the number straight in my head, and then a beeper goes off, or a forklift alarm starts, or somebody shifts a pallet near me, and my attention just jumps to that before I can stop it. Then when I go back to the count, I’ve lost where I was, and later I realize I entered the wrong numbers in inventory.\n\nSo I don’t know that his being gone makes my focus “better” in the way I’d hope. It might make the day feel less irritating at home, but I still can’t seem to keep my mind on one thing the way I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Other than your husband being home more, have you been feeling this same trouble focusing in situations that aren’t related to that change?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, the husband thing is part of what’s been making me feel off at home, but the focusing trouble doesn’t feel like it stays only in that lane, if that makes sense.\n\nThe clearest place it shows up is at work, at the warehouse, where it really matters because I’m counting and entering inventory numbers. I’ll be in the middle of a count, trying to keep the number in my head, and then something ordinary happens — a beeper goes off, or a forklift alarm starts, or someone moves a pallet nearby — and my attention just gets yanked over there. It’s not like I decide to look away. It just happens, and then when I try to go back, I’ve lost where I was.\n\nAnd that’s what’s scaring me a little, because then later I find out I put the wrong numbers into inventory. That’s really why I came in. It’s not just being irritated that my routine changed at home. It’s more like I can’t keep my mind on one thing the way I normally can, even in situations that don’t really have anything to do with him being home.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Like, I’ll be doing a count, and then a beeper goes off, or a forklift alarm, or somebody moves a pallet nearby, and my mind just snaps over to that. I don’t want it to sound like I don’t want him around, because that’s not really it. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d describe it as suddenly being on edge, exactly. I do notice the noises and movement around me — especially at work, because there’s always something beeping or rolling by or someone calling out — but I don’t know that I’m startled in the way you mean.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess the main thing is I just can’t keep my mind on one thing lately, and it’s starting to cause problems at work. Then I come back and I’ve lost my place, or I think I remember where I was, but later I realize I put the wrong numbers in. It’s embarrassing, because I know how to do my job. It’s not like I’m new there. I just… I can’t seem to stay on the count the way I used to, and that’s why I came in.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, did you also have a hard time staying focused?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I mean, it’s been a long time since I was a kid, so I don’t know how much I’m remembering right versus just filling in blanks. I don’t have a clear memory of teachers saying, like, “she can’t focus” or anything like that.\n\nI guess I was normal enough in school? I had subjects I liked and ones I didn’t, and if I was bored I probably looked out the window like any kid. But I can’t say there was some big problem with attention back then, not that I know of. This feels more like something I’m noticing now because it’s messing with my work.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it depressed, exactly. I mean, I get frustrated, definitely, especially when I mess something up that I know I should be able to do. That can make me feel pretty low in the moment, like I’m irritated with myself or kind of worn out by it.\n\nBut if you mean like feeling sad all the time or waking up every day with that heavy feeling… I’m not sure I’d say that’s been the main thing. I haven’t really been thinking of it as depression. It’s more that I’m bothered and stressed because I can’t keep my focus where it’s supposed to be, and then I start worrying about what people at work think.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot, even when there isn’t an immediate problem to deal with?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say I’m someone who’s worrying all day for no reason, exactly. I mean, I do worry about work right now because the mistakes are real, and I know if I keep losing my place or putting in the wrong numbers, that could become a problem. So that feels like there is something to worry about, if that makes sense.\n\nBut when there isn’t anything going on? I’m not sure. I don’t sit around thinking, like, “What if the whole world falls apart?” or anything like that. I can get stuck thinking things over, but it’s usually tied to something that actually happened, like replaying whether I counted something wrong or whether somebody noticed. It’s been about a month, maybe right around then, since my husband stopped working outside the home, and that kind of shifted the whole rhythm of the day.\n\nI don’t know if I’d call it “stressful” in the dramatic sense, like some huge upsetting event, but it did change things. Before, I had my usual pattern, and he had his, and now with him home more, everything feels a little different and less predictable. That’s around when I started really noticing the focus problems, especially at work. So… yes and no, I guess. Not a big crisis, but a change that threw off the normal routine.\n\nDoctor: Since your husband stopped working outside the home, have you felt more stressed than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so, though it’s hard for me to separate “stressed” from just everything feeling off, if that makes sense. It’s been going on for about a month, since he stopped working outside the home and our normal routine changed.  \n\nBefore that, we kind of had a rhythm, and I knew what the day was going to look like. Now it’s not bad exactly, but it’s different, and I do feel more tense or thrown off than usual because of it.\n\nDoctor: Besides at work, has this been causing problems in other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has. Work is the most obvious place because there are actual mistakes I can point to, like with inventory, and I’ve had to go back and double-check things with my supervisor after I realized something was off. That’s embarrassing, honestly, because I don’t like needing someone to recheck my work like that.\n\nBut it’s also been affecting home. My husband and I have been arguing more than usual, not huge blowups every time, but enough that the house feels tense. It’s like instead of settling into this new phase with him being home, we’re sort of walking around each other. I’ll get irritated, or he’ll say something, and then it turns into this back-and-forth when it probably didn’t need to. So yes, it’s not just work. It’s spilling over at home too.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your distress about your husband being home is stronger than you would expect for that change?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to judge that. Part of me thinks, well, it’s a big routine change, so maybe it makes sense that I’d feel thrown off. I was used to having the house and the day work a certain way, and now it’s just different all the time, even if nothing “bad” is happening.\n\nBut then another part of me wonders if I’m reacting more strongly than I should, because it’s not like he did something terrible by being home. I don’t want it to sound like I don’t want him around, because that’s not really it. It’s more like I feel more unsettled and irritated than I expected I would, and then I feel guilty about feeling that way.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Maybe a little stronger than I would’ve expected, but I can’t say for sure. It’s hard to measure that.\n\nDoctor: If your husband went back to working outside the home and your old routine returned, do you think these focus problems would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know if they would mostly go away. I think having the old routine back would probably help some, because I wouldn’t feel as thrown off at home, and maybe I’d go into work feeling less rattled.\n\nBut the focus problem itself is what really brought me in, because it’s been happening at the warehouse in a way that feels bigger than just being annoyed about my husband being home. Like, I’ll be doing a count, and then a beeper goes off, or a forklift alarm, or somebody moves a pallet nearby, and my mind just snaps over to that. Then I lose the thread of what number I was on, and later I realize I entered the wrong numbers in inventory.\n\nSo… maybe the routine going back would take some pressure off, yes. But I’m not sure it would fix it completely, because the problem is that I can’t seem to keep my mind on one thing the way I normally can.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sleeping poorly lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve been sleeping poorly, exactly. I mean, I haven’t been paying close attention to it the way I probably should. Some nights feel better than others, but I can’t point to a clear pattern or say, “Yes, I’m up all night,” or anything like that.\n\nI guess with everything feeling kind of tense at home and me worrying about mistakes at work, I do notice myself thinking about things when I’m trying to settle down, but whether that means my sleep is actually bad… I don’t know. I haven’t really tracked it. So I’d say I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: On days when your husband is out of the house for most of the day, is your focus any better?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but not enough that I’d say it solves it. If he’s out of the house most of the day, I do feel less crowded or less thrown off at home, so I might start the day a bit calmer. But the thing that’s really bothering me is what happens once I’m at the warehouse.\n\nLike I’ll be in the middle of counting something, and I’m trying to keep the number straight in my head, and then a beeper goes off, or a forklift alarm starts, or somebody shifts a pallet near me, and my attention just jumps to that before I can stop it. Then when I go back to the count, I’ve lost where I was, and later I realize I entered the wrong numbers in inventory.\n\nSo I don’t know that his being gone makes my focus “better” in the way I’d hope. It might make the day feel less irritating at home, but I still can’t seem to keep my mind on one thing the way I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Other than your husband being home more, have you been feeling this same trouble focusing in situations that aren’t related to that change?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. So I’d say I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: On days when your husband is out of the house for most of the day, is your focus any better?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but not enough that I’d say it solves it. If he’s out of the house most of the day, I do feel less crowded or less thrown off at home, so I might start the day a bit calmer. But the thing that’s really bothering me is what happens once I’m at the warehouse.\n\nLike I’ll be in the middle of counting something, and I’m trying to keep the number straight in my head, and then a beeper goes off, or a forklift alarm starts, or somebody shifts a pallet near me, and my attention just jumps to that before I can stop it. Then when I go back to the count, I’ve lost where I was, and later I realize I entered the wrong numbers in inventory.\n\nSo I don’t know that his being gone makes my focus “better” in the way I’d hope. It might make the day feel less irritating at home, but I still can’t seem to keep my mind on one thing the way I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Other than your husband being home more, have you been feeling this same trouble focusing in situations that aren’t related to that change?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. That’s really why I came in. It’s not just being irritated that my routine changed at home. It’s more like I can’t keep my mind on one thing the way I normally can, even in situations that don’t really have anything to do with him being home.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a head injury recently?\n\nPatient: No, not that I can think of. I haven’t hit my head or had a fall recently or anything like that. I mean, I do work around equipment and pallets and there’s always noise and things moving around, so I guess I’m aware of that kind of risk, but I don’t remember any bump or accident where I thought, “Oh, that could’ve caused something.” Nothing like that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, I’ve been frustrated and worried, especially when I mess something up at work or I feel like I’m not keeping up the way I should, and that can sit with me for a while. It’s more that I’m bothered by what’s happening and kind of uneasy about it.\n\nDoctor: Do ordinary noises or movement around you make you feel suddenly on edge, like you startle easily?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it as suddenly being on edge, exactly. I just… I can’t seem to stay on the count the way I used to, and that’s why I came in.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, did you also have a hard time staying focused?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I mean, it’s been a long time since I was a kid, so I don’t know how much I’m remembering right versus just filling in blanks. I don’t have a clear memory of teachers saying, like, “she can’t focus” or anything like that.\n\nI guess I was normal enough in school? I had subjects I liked and ones I didn’t, and if I was bored I probably looked out the window like any kid. But I can’t say there was some big problem with attention back then, not that I know of. This feels more like something I’m noticing now because it’s messing with my work.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it depressed, exactly. I mean, I get frustrated, definitely, especially when I mess something up that I know I should be able to do. That can make me feel pretty low in the moment, like I’m irritated with myself or kind of worn out by it.\n\nBut if you mean like feeling sad all the time or waking up every day with that heavy feeling… I’m not sure I’d say that’s been the main thing. I haven’t really been thinking of it as depression. It’s more that I’m bothered and stressed because I can’t keep my focus where it’s supposed to be, and then I start worrying about what people at work think.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot, even when there isn’t an immediate problem to deal with?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say I’m someone who’s worrying all day for no reason, exactly. I mean, I do worry about work right now because the mistakes are real, and I know if I keep losing my place or putting in the wrong numbers, that could become a problem. So that feels like there is something to worry about, if that makes sense.\n\nBut when there isn’t anything going on? I’m not sure. I don’t sit around thinking, like, “What if the whole world falls apart?” or anything like that. I can get stuck thinking things over, but it’s usually tied to something that actually happened, like replaying whether I counted something wrong or whether somebody noticed. I guess I’m not sure where the line is between normal worry and “worrying a lot.”\n\nDoctor: Did this trouble focusing start after something particularly stressful or upsetting happened in your life?\n\nPatient: I mean… the only thing I can really connect it to is that our routine changed at home. It’s been about a month, maybe right around then, since my husband stopped working outside the home, and that kind of shifted the whole rhythm of the day.\n\nI don’t know if I’d call it “stressful” in the dramatic sense, like some huge upsetting event, but it did change things. Before, I had my usual pattern, and he had his, and now with him home more, everything feels a little different and less predictable. That’s around when I started really noticing the focus problems, especially at work. So… yes and no, I guess. Not a big crisis, but a change that threw off the normal routine.\n\nDoctor: Since your husband stopped working outside the home, have you felt more stressed than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so, though it’s hard for me to separate “stressed” from just everything feeling off, if that makes sense. It’s been going on for about a month, since he stopped working outside the home and our normal routine changed.  \n\nBefore that, we kind of had a rhythm, and I knew what the day was going to look like. Now it’s not bad exactly, but it’s different, and I do feel more tense or thrown off than usual because of it.\n\nDoctor: Besides at work, has this been causing problems in other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has. Work is the most obvious place because there are actual mistakes I can point to, like with inventory, and I’ve had to go back and double-check things with my supervisor after I realized something was off. That’s embarrassing, honestly, because I don’t like needing someone to recheck my work like that.\n\nBut it’s also been affecting home. My husband and I have been arguing more than usual, not huge blowups every time, but enough that the house feels tense. It’s like instead of settling into this new phase with him being home, we’re sort of walking around each other. I’ll get irritated, or he’ll say something, and then it turns into this back-and-forth when it probably didn’t need to. So yes, it’s not just work. It’s spilling over at home too.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your distress about your husband being home is stronger than you would expect for that change?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to judge that. Part of me thinks, well, it’s a big routine change, so maybe it makes sense that I’d feel thrown off. I was used to having the house and the day work a certain way, and now it’s just different all the time, even if nothing “bad” is happening.\n\nBut then another part of me wonders if I’m reacting more strongly than I should, because it’s not like he did something terrible by being home. I don’t want it to sound like I don’t want him around, because that’s not really it. It’s more like I feel more unsettled and irritated than I expected I would, and then I feel guilty about feeling that way.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Maybe a little stronger than I would’ve expected, but I can’t say for sure. It’s hard to measure that.\n\nDoctor: If your husband went back to working outside the home and your old routine returned, do you think these focus problems would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know if they would mostly go away. I think having the old routine back would probably help some, because I wouldn’t feel as thrown off at home, and maybe I’d go into work feeling less rattled.\n\nBut the focus problem itself is what really brought me in, because it’s been happening at the warehouse in a way that feels bigger than just being annoyed about my husband being home. Like, I’ll be doing a count, and then a beeper goes off, or a forklift alarm, or somebody moves a pallet nearby, and my mind just snaps over to that. Then I lose the thread of what number I was on, and later I realize I entered the wrong numbers in inventory.\n\nSo… maybe the routine going back would take some pressure off, yes. But I’m not sure it would fix it completely, because the problem is that I can’t seem to keep my mind on one thing the way I normally can.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sleeping poorly lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve been sleeping poorly, exactly. I mean, I haven’t been paying close attention to it the way I probably should. Some nights feel better than others, but I can’t point to a clear pattern or say, “Yes, I’m up all night,” or anything like that.\n\nI guess with everything feeling kind of tense at home and me worrying about mistakes at work, I do notice myself thinking about things when I’m trying to settle down, but whether that means my sleep is actually bad… I don’t know. I haven’t really tracked it. So I’d say I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: On days when your husband is out of the house for most of the day, is your focus any better?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but not enough that I’d say it solves it. If he’s out of the house most of the day, I do feel less crowded or less thrown off at home, so I might start the day a bit calmer. But the thing that’s really bothering me is what happens once I’m at the warehouse.\n\nLike I’ll be in the middle of counting something, and I’m trying to keep the number straight in my head, and then a beeper goes off, or a forklift alarm starts, or somebody shifts a pallet near me, and my attention just jumps to that before I can stop it. Then when I go back to the count, I’ve lost where I was, and later I realize I entered the wrong numbers in inventory.\n\nSo I don’t know that his being gone makes my focus “better” in the way I’d hope. It might make the day feel less irritating at home, but I still can’t seem to keep my mind on one thing the way I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Other than your husband being home more, have you been feeling this same trouble focusing in situations that aren’t related to that change?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, the husband thing is part of what’s been making me feel off at home, but the focusing trouble doesn’t feel like it stays only in that lane, if that makes sense.\n\nThe clearest place it shows up is at work, at the warehouse, where it really matters because I’m counting and entering inventory numbers. I’ll be in the middle of a count, trying to keep the number in my head, and then something ordinary happens — a beeper goes off, or a forklift alarm starts, or someone moves a pallet nearby — and my attention just gets yanked over there. It’s not like I decide to look away. It just happens, and then when I try to go back, I’ve lost where I was.\n\nAnd that’s what’s scaring me a little, because then later I find out I put the wrong numbers into inventory. That’s really why I came in. It’s not just being irritated that my routine changed at home. It’s more like I can’t keep my mind on one thing the way I normally can, even in situations that don’t really have anything to do with him being home.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a head injury recently?\n\nPatient: No, not that I can think of. I haven’t hit my head or had a fall recently or anything like that. I mean, I do work around equipment and pallets and there’s always noise and things moving around, so I guess I’m aware of that kind of risk, but I don’t remember any bump or accident where I thought, “Oh, that could’ve caused something.” Nothing like that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, I’ve been frustrated and worried, especially when I mess something up at work or I feel like I’m not keeping up the way I should, and that can sit with me for a while. But “down most of the day nearly every day” — I don’t know. That sounds more constant than what I’ve noticed.\n\nI have had moments where I feel discouraged, like, “What is wrong with me, why can’t I just do this normally?” But I wouldn’t say I wake up every day feeling sad all day long. At least, not that I’ve been tracking it that way. It’s more that I’m bothered by what’s happening and kind of uneasy about it.\n\nDoctor: Do ordinary noises or movement around you make you feel suddenly on edge, like you startle easily?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it as suddenly being on edge, exactly. I do notice the noises and movement around me — especially at work, because there’s always something beeping or rolling by or someone calling out — but I don’t know that I’m startled in the way you mean.\n\nIt’s more like my attention gets pulled away, and then I’m annoyed because I lose my place. I don’t think I’m jumping out of my skin or feeling scared every time there’s a sound. I guess I’d have to pay more attention to that, because I haven’t really been thinking of it as being “on edge,” more just… distracted and thrown off.\n\nDoctor: When this happens, do you feel physically tense?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say physically tense exactly. I mean, sometimes if something interrupts me or there’s a lot going on, I might notice I’m kind of holding myself stiff, like my shoulders are up or I’m gripping what I’m doing, but I’m not sure if that’s tension or just me trying to concentrate and not make a mistake.\n\nI haven’t really paid close attention to my body in those moments, to be honest. Usually I’m more focused on, “Okay, where was I, what was I doing, did I miss something?” So I can’t say for sure that I feel tense every time. Maybe a little, sometimes, but it’s not something I’ve clearly noticed as a main thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel keyed up or restless?\n\nPatient: Yeah… restless might actually be a good word for it. Not all the time, but I’ve noticed it especially after work, which is kind of strange because normally after a shift I’d expect to be tired and just want to sit down.\n\nLately I’ll come home and I still feel kind of… buzzing, I guess. Like my body hasn’t gotten the message that the day is over. I’ll look at the couch and think I should relax, but sitting there feels almost impossible, like I need to get up and do something. So then I’ll start reorganizing cabinets, or sorting through bins in the garage, or moving things around that honestly probably don’t need to be moved right then.\n\nAnd it’s not like I planned to do some big project. It’s more like I walk in, and instead of winding down, I’m suddenly pulling things out of drawers and making piles. I don’t know if that counts as “keyed up,” but it feels like I have this extra motor running when I should be worn out.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

agitation.\n  manifestation: I've been taking on extra safety-improvement projects at work and signing up for more art workshops than I normally would, making lists and schedules like I'm running a sure I’d describe it as suddenly being on edge, exactly. I don’t think I’m jumping out of my skin or feeling scared every time there’s a sound. I guess I’d have to pay more attention to that, because I haven’t really been thinking of it as being “on edge,” more just… distracted and thrown off.\n\nDoctor: When this happens, do you feel physically tense?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say physically tense exactly. I don’t sit around thinking, like, “What if the whole world falls apart?” or anything like that. I guess I’m not sure where the line is between normal worry and “worrying a lot.”\n\nDoctor: Did this trouble focusing start after something particularly stressful or upsetting happened in your life?\n\nPatient: I mean… the only thing I can really connect it to is that our routine changed at home. It’s been about a month, maybe right around then, since my husband stopped working outside the home, and that kind of shifted the whole rhythm of the day.\n\nI don’t know if I’d call it “stressful” in the dramatic sense, like some huge upsetting event, but it did change things. Before, I had my usual pattern, and he had his, and now with him home more, everything feels a little different and less predictable. That’s around when I started really noticing the focus problems, especially at work. So… yes and no, I guess. It’s been going on for about a month, since he stopped working outside the home and our normal routine changed.  \n\nBefore that, we kind of had a rhythm, and I knew what the day was going to look like. Work is the most obvious place because there are actual mistakes I can point to, like with inventory, and I’ve had to go back and double-check things with my supervisor after I realized something was off. That’s embarrassing, honestly, because I don’t like needing someone to recheck my work like that.\n\nBut it’s also been affecting home. My husband and I have been arguing more than usual, not huge blowups every time, but enough that the house feels tense. It’s like instead of settling into this new phase with him being home, we’re sort of walking around each other. I’ll get irritated, or he’ll say something, and then it turns into this back-and-forth when it probably didn’t need to. So yes, it’s not just work. It’s spilling over at home too.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your distress about your husband being home is stronger than you would expect for that change?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to judge that. Part of me thinks, well, it’s a big routine change, so maybe it makes sense that I’d feel thrown off. I was used to having the house and the day work a certain way, and now it’s just different all the time, even if nothing “bad” is happening.\n\nBut then another part of me wonders if I’m reacting more strongly than I should, because it’s not like he did something terrible by being home. I don’t want it to sound like I don’t want him around, because that’s not really it. It’s more like I feel more unsettled and irritated than I expected I would, and then I feel guilty about feeling that way.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Maybe a little stronger than I would’ve expected, but I can’t say for sure. It’s hard to measure that.\n\nDoctor: If your husband went back to working outside the home and your old routine returned, do you think these focus problems would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know if they would mostly go away. I think having the old routine back would probably help some, because I wouldn’t feel as thrown off at home, and maybe I’d go into work feeling less rattled.\n\nBut the focus problem itself is what really brought me in, because it’s been happening at the warehouse in a way that feels bigger than just being annoyed about my husband being home. Like, I’ll be doing a count, and then a beeper goes off, or a forklift alarm, or somebody moves a pallet nearby, and my mind just snaps over to that. Then I lose the thread of what number I was on, and later I realize I entered the wrong numbers in inventory.\n\nSo… maybe the routine going back would take some pressure off, yes. But I’m not sure it would fix it completely, because the problem is that I can’t seem to keep my mind on one thing the way I normally can.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sleeping poorly lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve been sleeping poorly, exactly. I mean, I haven’t been paying close attention to it the way I probably should. I haven’t really tracked it. If he’s out of the house most of the day, I do feel less crowded or less thrown off at home, so I might start the day a bit calmer. But the thing that’s really bothering me is what happens once I’m at the warehouse.\n\nLike I’ll be in the middle of counting something, and I’m trying to keep the number straight in my head, and then a beeper goes off, or a forklift alarm starts, or somebody shifts a pallet near me, and my attention just jumps to that before I can stop it. Then when I go back to the count, I’ve lost where I was, and later I realize I entered the wrong numbers in inventory.\n\nSo I don’t know that his being gone makes my focus “better” in the way I’d hope. It might make the day feel less irritating at home, but I still can’t seem to keep my mind on one thing the way I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Other than your husband being home more, have you been feeling this same trouble focusing in situations that aren’t related to that change?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, the husband thing is part of what’s been making me feel off at home, but the focusing trouble doesn’t feel like it stays only in that lane, if that makes sense.\n\nThe clearest place it shows up is at work, at the warehouse, where it really matters because I’m counting and entering inventory numbers. I’ll be in the middle of a count, trying to keep the number in my head, and then something ordinary happens — a beeper goes off, or a forklift alarm starts, or someone moves a pallet nearby — and my attention just gets yanked over there. It’s not like I decide to look away. So that feels like there is something to worry about, if that makes sense.\n\nBut when there isn’t anything going on? I’m not sure. I don’t sit around thinking, like, “What if the whole world falls apart?” or anything like that. I can get stuck thinking things over, but it’s usually tied to something that actually happened, like replaying whether I counted something wrong or whether somebody noticed. I guess I’m not sure where the line is between normal worry and “worrying a lot.”\n\nDoctor: Did this trouble focusing start after something particularly stressful or upsetting happened in your life?\n\nPatient: I mean… the only thing I can really connect it to is that our routine changed at home. It’s been about a month, maybe right around then, since my husband stopped working outside the home, and that kind of shifted the whole rhythm of the day.\n\nI don’t know if I’d call it “stressful” in the dramatic sense, like some huge upsetting event, but it did change things. Before, I had my usual pattern, and he had his, and now with him home more, everything feels a little different and less predictable. That’s around when I started really noticing the focus problems, especially at work. So… yes and no, I guess. Not a big crisis, but a change that threw off the normal routine.\n\nDoctor: Since your husband stopped working outside the home, have you felt more stressed than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so, though it’s hard for me to separate “stressed” from just everything feeling off, if that makes sense. It’s been going on for about a month, since he stopped working outside the home and our normal routine changed.  \n\nBefore that, we kind of had a rhythm, and I knew what the day was going to look like. Now it’s not bad exactly, but it’s different, and I do feel more tense or thrown off than usual because of it.\n\nDoctor: Besides at work, has this been causing problems in other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has. Work is the most obvious place because there are actual mistakes I can point to, like with inventory, and I’ve had to go back and double-check things with my supervisor after I realized something was off. That’s embarrassing, honestly, because I don’t like needing someone to recheck my work like that.\n\nBut it’s also been affecting home. My husband and I have been arguing more than usual, not huge blowups every time, but enough that the house feels tense. It’s like instead of settling into this new phase with him being home, we’re sort of walking around each other. I’ll get irritated, or he’ll say something, and then it turns into this back-and-forth when it probably didn’t need to. So yes, it’s not just work. It’s spilling over at home too.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your distress about your husband being home is stronger than you would expect for that change?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to judge that. Part of me thinks, well, it’s a big routine change, so maybe it makes sense that I’d feel thrown off. I was used to having the house and the day work a certain way, and now it’s just different all the time, even if nothing “bad” is happening.\n\nBut then another part of me wonders if I’m reacting more strongly than I should, because it’s not like he did something terrible by being home. I don’t want it to sound like I don’t want him around, because that’s not really it. It’s more like I feel more unsettled and irritated than I expected I would, and then I feel guilty about feeling that way.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Maybe a little stronger than I would’ve expected, but I can’t say for sure. It’s hard to measure that.\n\nDoctor: If your husband went back to working outside the home and your old routine returned, do you think these focus problems would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know if they would mostly go away. I think having the old routine back would probably help some, because I wouldn’t feel as thrown off at home, and maybe I’d go into work feeling less rattled.\n\nBut the focus problem itself is what really brought me in, because it’s been happening at the warehouse in a way that feels bigger than just being annoyed about my husband being home. Like, I’ll be doing a count, and then a beeper goes off, or a forklift alarm, or somebody moves a pallet nearby, and my mind just snaps over to that. Then I lose the thread of what number I was on, and later I realize I entered the wrong numbers in inventory.\n\nSo… maybe the routine going back would take some pressure off, yes. But I’m not sure it would fix it completely, because the problem is that I can’t seem to keep my mind on one thing the way I normally can.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sleeping poorly lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve been sleeping poorly, exactly. I mean, I haven’t been paying close attention to it the way I probably should. Some nights feel better than others, but I can’t point to a clear pattern or say, “Yes, I’m up all night,” or anything like that.\n\nI guess with everything feeling kind of tense at home and me worrying about mistakes at work, I do notice myself thinking about things when I’m trying to settle down, but whether that means my sleep is actually bad… I don’t know. I haven’t really tracked it. So I’d say I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: On days when your husband is out of the house for most of the day, is your focus any better?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but not enough that I’d say it solves it. If he’s out of the house most of the day, I do feel less crowded or less thrown off at home, so I might start the day a bit calmer. But the thing that’s really bothering me is what happens once I’m at the warehouse.\n\nLike I’ll be in the middle of counting something, and I’m trying to keep the number straight in my head, and then a beeper goes off, or a forklift alarm starts, or somebody shifts a pallet near me, and my attention just jumps to that before I can stop it. Then when I go back to the count, I’ve lost where I was, and later I realize I entered the wrong numbers in inventory.\n\nSo I don’t know that his being gone makes my focus “better” in the way I’d hope. It might make the day feel less irritating at home, but I still can’t seem to keep my mind on one thing the way I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Other than your husband being home more, have you been feeling this same trouble focusing in situations that aren’t related to that change?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, the husband thing is part of what’s been making me feel off at home, but the focusing trouble doesn’t feel like it stays only in that lane, if that makes sense.\n\nThe clearest place it shows up is at work, at the warehouse, where it really matters because I’m counting and entering inventory numbers. I’ll be in the middle of a count, trying to keep the number in my head, and then something ordinary happens — a beeper goes off, or a forklift alarm starts, or someone moves a pallet nearby — and my attention just gets yanked over there. It’s not like I decide to look away. It just happens, and then when I try to go back, I’ve lost where I was.\n\nAnd that’s what’s scaring me a little, because then later I find out I put the wrong numbers into inventory. That’s really why I came in. It’s not just being irritated that my routine changed at home. It’s more like I can’t keep my mind on one thing the way I normally can, even in situations that don’t really have anything to do with him being home.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a head injury recently?\n\nPatient: No, not that I can think of. I haven’t hit my head or had a fall recently or anything like that. I mean, I do work around equipment and pallets and there’s always noise and things moving around, so I guess I’m aware of that kind of risk, but I don’t remember any bump or accident where I thought, “Oh, that could’ve caused something.” Nothing like that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, I’ve been frustrated and worried, especially when I mess something up at work or I feel like I’m not keeping up the way I should, and that can sit with me for a while. But “down most of the day nearly every day” — I don’t know. That sounds more constant than what I’ve noticed.\n\nI have had moments where I feel discouraged, like, “What is wrong with me, why can’t I just do this normally?” But I wouldn’t say I wake up every day feeling sad all day long. At least, not that I’ve been tracking it that way. It’s more that I’m bothered by what’s happening and kind of uneasy about it.\n\nDoctor: Do ordinary noises or movement around you make you feel suddenly on edge, like you startle easily?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it as suddenly being on edge, exactly. I do notice the noises and movement around me — especially at work, because there’s always something beeping or rolling by or someone calling out — but I don’t know that I’m startled in the way you mean.\n\nIt’s more like my attention gets pulled away, and then I’m annoyed because I lose my place. I don’t think I’m jumping out of my skin or feeling scared every time there’s a sound. I guess I’d have to pay more attention to that, because I haven’t really been thinking of it as being “on edge,” more just… distracted and thrown off.\n\nDoctor: When this happens, do you feel physically tense?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say physically tense exactly. Usually I’m more focused on, “Okay, where was I, what was I doing, did I miss something?” So I can’t say for sure that I feel tense every time. Maybe a little, sometimes, but it’s not something I’ve clearly noticed as a main thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel keyed up or restless?\n\nPatient: Yeah… restless might actually be a good word for it. Not all the time, but I’ve noticed it especially after work, which is kind of strange because normally after a shift I’d expect to be tired and just want to sit down.\n\nLately I’ll come home and I still feel kind of… buzzing, I guess. Like my body hasn’t gotten the message that the day is over. I’ll look at the couch and think I should relax, but sitting there feels almost impossible, like I need to get up and do something. So then I’ll start reorganizing cabinets, or sorting through bins in the garage, or moving things around that honestly probably don’t need to be moved right then.\n\nAnd it’s not like I planned to do some big project. It’s more like I walk in, and instead of winding down, I’m suddenly pulling things out of drawers and making piles. I don’t know if that counts as “keyed up,” but it feels like I have this extra motor running when I should be worn out.\n\nDoctor: Have you needed less sleep than usual lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I haven’t really been tracking it closely, like writing down what time I go to bed or anything. Some nights I feel like I don’t settle down as quickly as I should, but I couldn’t honestly say whether I’m sleeping a lot less overall.\n\nI still go through the usual routine — getting ready for bed, trying to lie down — but whether I’m actually needing less sleep, I don’t know. Before, I had my usual pattern, and he had his, and now with him home more, everything feels a little different and less predictable. That’s around when I started really noticing the focus problems, especially at work. So… yes and no, I guess. Not a big crisis, but a change that threw off the normal routine.\n\nDoctor: Since your husband stopped working outside the home, have you felt more stressed than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so, though it’s hard for me to separate “stressed” from just everything feeling off, if that makes sense. It’s been going on for about a month, since he stopped working outside the home and our normal routine changed.  \n\nBefore that, we kind of had a rhythm, and I knew what the day was going to look like. Now it’s not bad exactly, but it’s different, and I do feel more tense or thrown off than usual because of it.\n\nDoctor: Besides at work, has this been causing problems in other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has. Work is the most obvious place because there are actual mistakes I can point to, like with inventory, and I’ve had to go back and double-check things with my supervisor after I realized something was off. That’s embarrassing, honestly, because I don’t like needing someone to recheck my work like that.\n\nBut it’s also been affecting home. My husband and I have been arguing more than usual, not huge blowups every time, but enough that the house feels tense. It’s like instead of settling into this new phase with him being home, we’re sort of walking around each other. I’ll get irritated, or he’ll say something, and then it turns into this back-and-forth when it probably didn’t need to. So yes, it’s not just work. It’s spilling over at home too.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your distress about your husband being home is stronger than you would expect for that change?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to judge that. Part of me thinks, well, it’s a big routine change, so maybe it makes sense that I’d feel thrown off. I was used to having the house and the day work a certain way, and now it’s just different all the time, even if nothing “bad” is happening.\n\nBut then another part of me wonders if I’m reacting more strongly than I should, because it’s not like he did something terrible by being home. I don’t want it to sound like I don’t want him around, because that’s not really it. It’s more like I feel more unsettled and irritated than I expected I would, and then I feel guilty about feeling that way.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Maybe a little stronger than I would’ve expected, but I can’t say for sure. It’s hard to measure that.\n\nDoctor: If your husband went back to working outside the home and your old routine returned, do you think these focus problems would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know if they would mostly go away. I think having the old routine back would probably help some, because I wouldn’t feel as thrown off at home, and maybe I’d go into work feeling less rattled.\n\nBut the focus problem itself is what really brought me in, because it’s been happening at the warehouse in a way that feels bigger than just being annoyed about my husband being home. Like, I’ll be doing a count, and then a beeper goes off, or a forklift alarm, or somebody moves a pallet nearby, and my mind just snaps over to that. Then I lose the thread of what number I was on, and later I realize I entered the wrong numbers in inventory.\n\nSo… maybe the routine going back would take some pressure off, yes. But I’m not sure it would fix it completely, because the problem is that I can’t seem to keep my mind on one thing the way I normally can.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sleeping poorly lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve been sleeping poorly, exactly. I mean, I haven’t been paying close attention to it the way I probably should. Some nights feel better than others, but I can’t point to a clear pattern or say, “Yes, I’m up all night,” or anything like that.\n\nI guess with everything feeling kind of tense at home and me worrying about mistakes at work, I do notice myself thinking about things when I’m trying to settle down, but whether that means my sleep is actually bad… I don’t know. I haven’t really tracked it. So I’d say I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: On days when your husband is out of the house for most of the day, is your focus any better?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but not enough that I’d say it solves it. If he’s out of the house most of the day, I do feel less crowded or less thrown off at home, so I might start the day a bit calmer. But the thing that’s really bothering me is what happens once I’m at the warehouse.\n\nLike I’ll be in the middle of counting something, and I’m trying to keep the number straight in my head, and then a beeper goes off, or a forklift alarm starts, or somebody shifts a pallet near me, and my attention just jumps to that before I can stop it. Then when I go back to the count, I’ve lost where I was, and later I realize I entered the wrong numbers in inventory.\n\nSo I don’t know that his being gone makes my focus “better” in the way I’d hope. It might make the day feel less irritating at home, but I still can’t seem to keep my mind on one thing the way I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Other than your husband being home more, have you been feeling this same trouble focusing in situations that aren’t related to that change?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, the husband thing is part of what’s been making me feel off at home, but the focusing trouble doesn’t feel like it stays only in that lane, if that makes sense.\n\nThe clearest place it shows up is at work, at the warehouse, where it really matters because I’m counting and entering inventory numbers. I’ll be in the middle of a count, trying to keep the number in my head, and then something ordinary happens — a beeper goes off, or a forklift alarm starts, or someone moves a pallet nearby — and my attention just gets yanked over there. It’s not like I decide to look away. It just happens, and then when I try to go back, I’ve lost where I was.\n\nAnd that’s what’s scaring me a little, because then later I find out I put the wrong numbers into inventory. That’s really why I came in. It’s not just being irritated that my routine changed at home. It’s more like I can’t keep my mind on one thing the way I normally can, even in situations that don’t really have anything to do with him being home.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a head injury recently?\n\nPatient: No, not that I can think of. I haven’t hit my head or had a fall recently or anything like that. I mean, I do work around equipment and pallets and there’s always noise and things moving around, so I guess I’m aware of that kind of risk, but I don’t remember any bump or accident where I thought, “Oh, that could’ve caused something.” Nothing like that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, I’ve been frustrated and worried, especially when I mess something up at work or I feel like I’m not keeping up the way I should, and that can sit with me for a while. But “down most of the day nearly every day” — I don’t know. That sounds more constant than what I’ve noticed.\n\nI have had moments where I feel discouraged, like, “What is wrong with me, why can’t I just do this normally?” But I wouldn’t say I wake up every day feeling sad all day long. At least, not that I’ve been tracking it that way. It’s more that I’m bothered by what’s happening and kind of uneasy about it.\n\nDoctor: Do ordinary noises or movement around you make you feel suddenly on edge, like you startle easily?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it as suddenly being on edge, exactly. I do notice the noises and movement around me — especially at work, because there’s always something beeping or rolling by or someone calling out — but I don’t know that I’m startled in the way you mean.\n\nIt’s more like my attention gets pulled away, and then I’m annoyed because I lose my place. I don’t think I’m jumping out of my skin or feeling scared every time there’s a sound. I guess I’d have to pay more attention to that, because I haven’t really been thinking of it as being “on edge,” more just… distracted and thrown off.\n\nDoctor: When this happens, do you feel physically tense?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say physically tense exactly. I mean, sometimes if something interrupts me or there’s a lot going on, I might notice I’m kind of holding myself stiff, like my shoulders are up or I’m gripping what I’m doing, but I’m not sure if that’s tension or just me trying to concentrate and not make a mistake.\n\nI haven’t really paid close attention to my body in those moments, to be honest. Usually I’m more focused on, “Okay, where was I, what was I doing, did I miss something?” So I can’t say for sure that I feel tense every time. Maybe a little, sometimes, but it’s not something I’ve clearly noticed as a main thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel keyed up or restless?\n\nPatient: Yeah… restless might actually be a good word for it. Not all the time, but I’ve noticed it especially after work, which is kind of strange because normally after a shift I’d expect to be tired and just want to sit down.\n\nLately I’ll come home and I still feel kind of… buzzing, I guess. Like my body hasn’t gotten the message that the day is over. I’ll look at the couch and think I should relax, but sitting there feels almost impossible, like I need to get up and do something. So then I’ll start reorganizing cabinets, or sorting through bins in the garage, or moving things around that honestly probably don’t need to be moved right then.\n\nAnd it’s not like I planned to do some big project. It’s more like I walk in, and instead of winding down, I’m suddenly pulling things out of drawers and making piles. I don’t know if that counts as “keyed up,” but it feels like I have this extra motor running when I should be worn out.\n\nDoctor: Have you needed less sleep than usual lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I haven’t really been tracking it closely, like writing down what time I go to bed or anything. Some nights I feel like I don’t settle down as quickly as I should, but I couldn’t honestly say whether I’m sleeping a lot less overall.\n\nI still go through the usual routine — getting ready for bed, trying to lie down — but whether I’m actually needing less sleep, I don’t know. I think I’d have to pay more attention to it for a few nights to give you a better answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually energetic?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so… I mean, “energetic” sounds almost positive, like I’m excited to go do something fun, and it doesn’t always feel like that. But after my shifts lately, instead of being wiped out the way I usually am, I’ll come home and still feel like I’m buzzing.\n\nLike, I’ll think, okay, I should sit down on the couch for a minute, but then sitting there feels almost impossible. I’ll get right back up and start doing things — reorganizing cabinets, pulling stuff out, sorting bins in the garage, moving things around. Sometimes I’ll be halfway into it before I even really decide to do it.\n\nIt’s not exactly that I have some grand plan. It’s more like I have all this energy still running through me and I need to put it somewhere, even if it’s just making piles or rearranging things that probably could’ve waited.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Yeah… some days, yes. I wouldn’t say I’m walking around laughing or anything like that, but there are days where I feel almost too upbeat, like I’ve got this big wave of confidence and everything seems doable all at once.

And then afterward I’m like… why did I react that strongly? So it’s not just cheerful exactly. It’s more like I feel really up and capable, but also kind of touchy if someone slows me down or doubts what I’m doing.

In the moment it feels completely reasonable, like of course I can handle all of it.\n\nBut it can flip kind of fast, too. If my husband says something like, “Do you really need to do that right now?” or even just questions one of my ideas, I’ll snap at him over something small. And then afterward I’m like… why did I react that strongly? So it’s not just cheerful exactly. It’s more like I feel really up and capable, but also kind of touchy if someone slows me down or doubts what I’m doing."
